### **@duranxv** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mk4vcHtawSo&lc=UgwNiDWCV5PyS75L05l4AaABAg)

Great analysis Professor, there are a few things to consider with your predictions though.  I'm by no means certain of any outcome, but food for thought:

1) From a sheer numbers/population perspective, Israel just does not have the population or military size to control the Middle East for any extended period of time.  Overreliance on high-tech equipment has already proven to be a vulnerability, as evidenced by Oct 7th and low tech missile/drone strikes increasingly penetrating the Iron Dome

2) Israel is still very dependent on aid and equipment mainly from the United States.  If those flows are curtailed (ex. USA suffers a significant defeat and the America First Movement continues to build momentum for full isolationism), this will significantly limit Israel's military capabilities unless they seek an alliance with China or Russia

3) Israeli society is becoming increasingly divided between secular and religious lines.  The ultra orthodox do not wish to fight in the military, and as their numbers grow rapidly, this will create additional political and societal pressure which may limit their military capabilities and eventually even their ability to innovate in high tech.  Secular Israeli's will not take this lying down, especially if there's more pressure to replace losses and expand the military

4) Israel's economy cannot handle extended conflict, its economy is already buckling under the pressure, and they've seen a flight of talent to other countries, as well as decreased foreign investment.  Forced military service often drains economic output, especially if you're drafting working people back into the military to fight.  All IDF soldiers are on reserve and can be recalled at anytime to serve on a battlefield

5) For Iran to eventually decide to work with Israel would be highly unlikely if the current regime stays in power.  It would actually threaten their legitimacy given that they used Israel as a common foe for decades to motivate their population to support the government. Plus, we are underestimating the desire for revenge of generations of families who have been killed by US/Israeli strikes both in Iran and other parts of the Middle East

6) I understand that there are religious forces at work to bring about the coming of the Messiah and the end of the world.  However, the question to ask is this:  "End of the world for whom?"  -  There's been end of the world prophecies that technically came true in the past, and yet it only resulted in the end of a certain civilization or empire rather than the entire world

7) We are still assuming that the Middle East will continue to be an important region for oil in the next 20-30 years, but this bars any technological breakthoughs, Arctic expansion and other factors which may start to minimize the strategic importance of this region.  Even if Israel gets what it wants, will it simply be an Empire of Ash?

8) We have to also take into account Shia/Sunni divides.  Shiites are very hierarchical, and given that Iran is surrounded by Sunni neighbors, it's own strength and sovereignty is a matter of survival.  Even if Iran decides to normalize with Israel, this won't last long.  Just like Israel believes in a "Greater Israel" - there is a desire in Iran to bring back the old glory of Persia.  You have two competing "empire narratives"

### **@samiyarsaviz**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mk4vcHtawSo&lc=Ugx0z9U4wpQzL4E9ZAV4AaABAg)

@PredictiveHistory

Professor, this is just my personal opinion.

First, many of the forecasts you make are initially presented almost like firm statements, and only later reframed as “predictions.” That presentation matters, because it can come across as signaling that this is definitely what will happen, rather than simply one possible scenario. The world is not black and white, and these issues are far too complex for that kind of certainty.

Second, many of your assumptions, especially regarding a possible Iran–US war, seem to rest on hundreds of smaller assumptions, even though the probability of many of them happening is not that high. When looking at broad macroeconomics and geopolitics, there are also other major forces at play, such as mass psychology, public pressure, and herd behavior, all of which can bend the “rules” of game theory on paper.

For example, on paper Iran reportedly has around 700K active-duty troops, and that number can rise to around 1.6M if we include what are described as on-call soldiers and reserve forces. But Iran also has a population of around 85M+, and the majority are already under severe economic pressure. According to Mohammad-Bagher Ghalibaf, the current Speaker of the Parliament of Iran, around 80% of people are under the minimum poverty line. If that is true, then we are talking about 65+ of millions of people under extreme pressure.

That is why building so much of the analysis around one war scenario may not be the strongest approach. A deal with the US is only one possibility. Internal collapse, regime change, or a completely different regional realignment are also possible.

So from my point of view, the probability of a full-scale internal revolution by the people of Iran may actually be higher than the scenario in which Iran reaches a deal with the US, the US leaves the Middle East, Iran becomes the one dominant power in the region, and Israel is then forced into a deal with Iran. In other words, even the survival of the current regime is itself questionable, yet much of your prediction seems to be built on the assumption that this regime remains stable enough for all of those external scenarios to happen.

With all that being said, the GCC-era style of global certainty is already over. Around the world, leaders seem to have understood that they need solutions that make them less dependent on other countries. There is even a possibility that the EU may eventually make some kind of arrangement or limited alignment with Russia, maybe not willingly, but out of necessity and survival.

So yes, I agree with you that the world has changed and that the old rules do not stand anymore. But the current predictions still do not seem to have very solid support. They are probabilities, scenarios, and event-analysis models — not certainties. And the way these scenarios are presented to the public is what really matters.

### **@k98killer**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mk4vcHtawSo&lc=UgxyjZwWwUcQtG6f-LV4AaABAg)

Unfortunately, Mr. Jiang is really misinformed on the topic of military capabilities. The US and Israel have intercepted something like 95-99% of Iranian drones and missiles. By comparison, Iran has intercepted well less than 1% of American and Israeli munitions. We are not throwing $3 million PAC-2/3 missiles at $30k Shahed drones; the US has deployed Merops interceptors ($10-20k each), reusable Coyote drones with microwave weapons ($100-200k each), A-10 Warthogs (~$15-20k per interception), and C-RAMs (~$5-10k/interception) as part of their new layered defense strategy (LIDS). The idea that we are spending 100x for every intercepted munition is a persistent and inaccurate myth. Patriot and THAAD do have a failure rate, especially against the most advanced Iranian missiles, but Iran has lost ~70% of their missile launchers and managed to take out only a much smaller fraction of American and Israeli military assets. The numbers are overwhelmingly in our favor, not theirs.

The nature of perception in a hypercritical media landscape is that 500 successes will be ignored for every 1 error upon which people fixate. That misperception has an exponentially higher failure rate than American air defense systems.
